=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given two arrays, nums and target.
In a single operation, you may increment any element of nums by 1.
Return the minimum number of operations required so that each element in target has at least one multiple in nums.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [1,2,3], target = [4]
Output: 1
Explanation:
The minimum number of operations required to satisfy the condition is 1.

Increment 3 to 4 with just one operation, making 4 a multiple of itself.


Example 2:

Input: nums = [8,4], target = [10,5]
Output: 2
Explanation:
The minimum number of operations required to satisfy the condition is 2.

Increment 8 to 10 with 2 operations, making 10 a multiple of both 5 and 10.


Example 3:

Input: nums = [7,9,10], target = [7]
Output: 0
Explanation:
Target 7 already has a multiple in nums, so no additional operations are needed.

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 5 * 10^4
1 <= target.length <= 4
target.length <= nums.length
1 <= nums[i], target[i] <= 10^4

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minimumIncrements(self, nums: List[int], target: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

for cur in range(M):

        sub = available

            nm = cur | sub
            ndp[nm] = min(ndp[nm], dp[cur] + costs[sub])
            sub = (sub - 1) & available

Thus DP can be optimized:
dp = [INF]*M; dp[0]=0
for a in nums:
    costs = [0]*M
    for mask in range(1,M):
       L=lcms[mask]
       r=a%L
       costs[mask]=0 if r==0 else L-r
    ndp = dp[:]  # not use
    for cur in range(M):
       base = dp[cur]
       if base >= INF: continue
       avail = full ^ cur
       sub = avail
       while sub:
           nm = cur | sub
           val = base + costs[sub]
           if val < ndp[nm]:
               ndp[nm] = val
           sub = (sub - 1) & avail
    dp = ndp
return dp[full]
But note: If we use ndp = dp[:] and then iterate cur over dp (old), transitions from old states only. Good. But what about using current element to cover new targets from a state that itself was achieved by using current element? Not allowed; we use old dp, so no. Good.

Let's draft code:

from typing import List
from math import gcd

class Solution:
    def minimumIncrements(self, nums: List[int], target: List[int]) -> int:
        m = len(target)
        M = 1 << m
        full = M - 1
        
        lcm = [1] * M
        for mask in range(1, M):
            # compute lcm from lcm of mask without lowest set bit?
            # Could compute incremental:
            # lsb = mask & -mask
            # i = lsb.bit_length() - 1
            # prev = mask ^ lsb
            # lcm[mask] = lcm[prev] * target[i] // gcd(lcm[prev], target[i])
            # This is efficient.
        for mask in range(1, M):
            lsb = mask & -mask
            i = lsb.bit_length() - 1
            prev = mask ^ lsb
            lcm[mask] = lcm[prev] * target[i] // gcd(lcm[prev], target[i])
        
        INF = 10**18
        dp = [INF] * M
        dp[0] = 0
        
        for a in nums:
            costs = [0] * M
            for mask in range(1, M):
                L = lcm[mask]
                r = a % L
                if r:
                    costs[mask] = L - r
                # else 0
            ndp = dp[:]  # skip this element
            for cur in range(M):
                base = dp[cur]
                if base >= INF:
                    continue
                avail = full ^ cur
                sub = avail
                while sub:
                    nm = cur | sub
                    val = base + costs[sub]
                    if val < ndp[nm]:
                        ndp[nm] = val
                    sub = (sub - 1) & avail
            dp = ndp
        
        return dp[full]

Need think about lcm incremental with mask bit order. Suppose mask has bits 0 and 1. mask=3, lsb=1, i=0, prev=2. lcm[2] computed target[1]. lcm[3]=lcm[2]*target[0]//gcd(lcm[2], target[0]). This equals lcm(target0,target1). Good. If mask=5 (bits0,2), lsb=1, prev=4 lcm target2, combine target0. Good. If lsb is not the "last" bit, but lcm commutative. Good.

Let's consider if there is a potential bug in DP with `ndp = dp[:]` and then updating from `dp` only. Suppose current element can cover sub1 from cur0, resulting in state cur1. Then the same current element cannot also cover sub2 from cur1. We don't allow because we use old dp. Good. But what if the optimal use of current element is to cover a subset that is not disjoint from cur but includes some already covered targets, and the cost for new targets alone is lower, but the final value for new targets alone might not be a multiple of the already covered targets, and maybe the already covered target was covered by an element that we could then "free" by not incrementing it? Wait, DP state `cur` represents targets covered by previous elements. It does not track which elements were used or their costs beyond min. If we use current element to cover new targets only, previous elements remain as they were. If instead we used current element to cover new targets plus some already covered targets, we might be able to avoid incrementing the previous element that covered those already covered targets, reducing cost. Ah! This is important. Let's examine.

Our DP processes elements sequentially and maintains dp[mask] = min cost using processed elements to cover mask. It assumes each processed element either unused or assigned to some subset. If we later use a new element to cover targets already covered, we cannot "unuse" previous elements; dp[cur] already paid for covering cur. But in an optimal global solution, we might choose to cover a target with a later element and not use an earlier element for it. However, because DP processes elements in order, when we are at later element, dp[cur] may have been achieved using earlier elements. If we use later element to cover some of cur plus new targets, we could potentially reduce cost by not having used earlier elements for those cur targets. But DP state doesn't allow removing previous coverage. Is this a problem?

Let's think carefully. The DP is a standard 0/1 knapsack over items, where each item can cover a subset. We process items in order. dp[mask] after processing first k items is the min cost using any subset of those k items to cover exactly mask (or at least mask). When processing item k+1, we consider transitions from dp[mask] (using previous items) plus item k+1 covering sub. This covers mask ∪ sub. If item k+1 covers some targets already in mask, we don't consider because sub disjoint. But what if optimal solution uses item k+1 to cover targets in mask and not use some previous item that dp[mask] used? Then dp[mask] might be higher than necessary if we knew item k+1 would cover those targets. However, dp[mask] is the minimum cost to cover mask using previous items. If optimal uses item k+1 to cover some targets in mask, then the previous items only need to cover mask \ (targets covered by k+1). That is a different state, not mask. Our transition from that smaller state with sub = targets covered by k+1 (disjoint) covers exactly that. So we don't need to transition from mask with overlapping sub. We can transition from mask without those targets. Since dp[smaller_mask] <= dp[mask]? Actually dp[smaller_mask] is min cost to cover fewer targets, likely <= dp[mask]. Then adding item k+1 to cover the difference yields the optimal. Thus overlapping transitions are not needed; the standard DP with disjoint new targets from any previous state covers all possibilities because the set of targets covered by item k+1 can be considered as sub, and previous items cover the rest (cur). If item k+1 covers some targets that in another representation were covered by previous items, we can instead represent those targets as covered by item k+1 and previous items cover the rest. So DP over masks with disjoint sub is complete. Good.

But let's examine the "freeing previous elements" issue more concretely. Suppose previous element A was used to cover target X with cost 5. Later element B can cover X and Y with cost 6. If we use B to cover X and Y, we don't need A. In DP, before B, dp[{X}] =5. Transition from dp[0] with sub={X,Y} cost6 gives dp[{X,Y}]=6, which is better. So no need to transition from dp[{X}] with overlapping sub. Good.

What if previous dp[mask] was achieved using some elements that we might want to "reassign" because B covers some of mask but not all, and the previous elements used for mask could instead cover other targets? DP state doesn't track assignment, but because items are processed sequentially, any reassignment across items can be represented by choosing different subsets for items in the order. The DP over items with all possible subsets for each item is a standard set cover DP; it doesn't need to remove coverage because the order of items in the solution is fixed but arbitrary. At each item, we decide which subset of targets it covers. The union after all items is full. The cost is sum. This is exactly a weighted set cover over items. The DP recurrence `dp_new[mask | sub] = min(dp_old[mask] + cost_i(sub))` with sub any subset (not necessarily disjoint) is the standard item DP. If we restrict sub disjoint from mask, is it still equivalent? In the standard recurrence, if sub overlaps mask, then `mask | sub = mask | (sub \ mask)`, and cost_i(sub) >= cost_i(sub \ mask) because lcm monotonic. Thus overlapping transitions are dominated by using sub' = sub \ mask. So restricting to disjoint sub is valid. This also addresses freeing: if item i covers some targets already in mask, using it for only the new targets is no more expensive and covers the same union. It doesn't cover the already covered targets, but they are already covered. So no need. Thus DP with disjoint sub is complete.

But wait, what if cost_i(sub) is not >= cost_i(sub \ mask) due to lcm monotonic? We proved yes. Let's double-check with a concrete case where sub has two targets A,B and sub\mask = B. lcm(A,B) is multiple of lcm(B). The set of multiples of lcm(A,B) is subset of multiples of lcm(B). The minimum multiple of lcm(A,B) >= a is >= minimum multiple of lcm(B) >= a. So cost_i(sub) >= cost_i(B). Yes. Thus dominated.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "dp[mask]" representing "at least mask" vs "exactly mask". Our transitions with disjoint sub and union produce at least mask. If an element covers extra targets, we include them in sub. If dp[mask] was achieved with elements that also cover extra targets beyond mask, dp would have been updated to larger mask at same or lower cost? Actually if an element covers extra, the transition with larger sub would have covered them. So dp[mask] is min cost to cover at least mask? We initialize dp[0]=0 and update unions. We never store a state with extra targets not in mask because union includes them. So dp[mask] is min cost to cover exactly the set mask (or at least mask but no extra tracked). Since covering extra is beneficial, the final full mask is what matters. For intermediate states, if a solution covers mask plus extra, it would be stored in dp[mask∪extra], not dp[mask]. So dp[mask] is min cost to cover all targets in mask and possibly no extra? Actually if a solution covers extra, it also covers mask, so it is a valid way to cover mask. Should dp[mask] include it? In standard DP, dp[mask] is min cost to cover at least mask, and a solution covering extra is also valid for mask. But our update only sets dp[union], not dp[subset]. So dp[mask] might not include solutions that cover extra. Does this matter for transitions? If we have a solution covering extra, we can use it as state for larger mask, not for smaller. When combining with future elements, starting from larger mask is always at least as good as starting from smaller mask because covering more targets cannot hurt; but cost may be higher. However, if a solution covers extra at same or lower cost, dp[larger] captures it. If it covers extra at higher cost, it might not be useful for covering just smaller mask because you could have chosen not to cover extra at lower cost (by using smaller sub). Since cost monotonic, covering extra with same element costs >= covering subset. So any solution covering extra is dominated for the purpose of covering the smaller mask by the solution where that element covers only the subset (if the extra targets are not needed yet). But if the extra targets are needed eventually, then starting from larger mask is better. Thus storing only exact union is fine.

Let's test a scenario: Element A covers targets {1,2} with cost5, but we only need target1 now; later element B covers target2 with cost1. Total6. Alternative: A covers target1 cost? cost to cover {1} maybe2, B covers target2 cost1 total3. Since cost({1}) <= cost({1,2}), the exact DP will find cheaper. So no need to store covering extra for smaller mask.

Thus DP correct.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "nums" and "target" variable names shadow? no.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from typing import List" in code block before class; okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "from math import gcd" in code block; okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "target.length <= nums.length" but if target length is 0? no.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "nums" length 5e4 and target length 4, but if all target values are 1, DP will set dp[full]=0 after first element and then continue
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List
from math import gcd

class Solution:
    def minimumIncrements(self, nums: List[int], target: List[int]) -> int:
        m = len(target)
        M = 1 << m
        full = M - 1
        INF = 10**30

        # lcm[mask] = lcm of target values in mask
        lcm = [1] * M
        for mask in range(1, M):
            lsb = mask & -mask
            i = lsb.bit_length() - 1
            prev = mask ^ lsb
            lcm[mask] = lcm[prev] * target[i] // gcd(lcm[prev], target[i])

        # Precompute all non-empty subsets of currently uncovered targets.
        submasks = [[] for _ in range(M)]
        for cur in range(M):
            avail = full ^ cur
            sub = avail
            while sub:
                submasks[cur].append(sub)
                sub = (sub - 1) & avail

        dp = [INF] * M
        dp[0] = 0

        for a in nums:
            # cost[mask] = min increments to make a a multiple of lcm[mask]
            cost = [0] * M
            for mask in range(1, M):
                L = lcm[mask]
                r = a % L
                if r:
                    cost[mask] = L - r

            ndp = dp[:]  # Option: do not use this nums element.

            for cur in range(M):
                base = dp[cur]
                if base >= INF:
                    continue

                for sub in submasks[cur]:
                    nm = cur | sub
                    val = base + cost[sub]
                    if val < ndp[nm]:
                        ndp[nm] = val

            dp = ndp
            if dp[full] == 0:
                return 0

        return dp[full]
```
